  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch — Death-proximal labeling negative result 补进 §3.6]\n\n紧急补丁，尽快完成。Death-proximal labeling 实验结果已出（全部负面），需补进 §3.6 \"Router Training: A Negative Result\"。\n\n**在 Interpretation A/B 讨论之后、\"Regardless of interpretation\" 句之前，插入以下内容**：\n\n\"To directly test Interpretation A, we trained routers on death-proximal subsets—retaining only steps within k∈{1,3,5,10} of a fatal action. [Director 指令] [Patch — Death-proximal labeling negative result 补进 §3.6]

紧急补丁，尽快完成。Death-proximal labeling 实验结果已出（全部负面），需补进 §3.6 "Router Training: A Negative Result"。

**在 Interpretation A/B 讨论之后、"Regardless of interpretation" 句之前，插入以下内容**：

"To directly test Interpretation A, we trained routers on death-proximal subsets—retaining only steps within k∈{1,3,5,10} of a fatal action. If label dilution were the bottleneck, concentrating training on informative steps should improve accuracy. Instead, all configurations yield internal recall of 0% and balanced accuracy of 50%, identical to the full-dataset baseline. The failure is structural: death-asymmetry episodes are inherently single-class (all labeled deterministic), so filtering uninformative steps cannot create internal-preferred training signal. Combined with the data scaling ablation (§3.7, 25%–800% with no improvement), this confirms that routing signal is absent at the step level, favoring Interpretation B."

**同时更新 Interpretation B 的总结句**，从"Both interpretations are consistent with our data"改为：
"Death-proximal filtering and data scaling ablation both favor Interpretation B: routing signal is genuinely task-type-level, not step-level learnable."

**删除** "Death-proximal labeling—training only on steps near death events—could distinguish them (see §7 Future Work)" 这句话（实验已做完，不再是 future work）。

确保页面仍 ≤8 页。如超页，压缩 §3.6 中其他冗余措辞。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GpBVBwvwf2H5uZyTpUnvYQ`</summary>

```text
107	\subsection{Router Training: A Negative Result}
108	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
109	
110	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. The near-chance step-level accuracy admits two interpretations: (A)~episode-level labels projected onto steps create ${\sim}$88\% uninformative signal (60.3\% pre-event neutral, 27.4\% post-event), drowning learnable patterns in noise; (B)~routing signal is genuinely task-type-level---there is no step-level pattern to learn. Evidence favors a mixture: directional conflict is only 0.3\% of 2{,}040 training steps (labels are directionally correct in 99.7\% of informative steps), but the granularity mismatch leaves minimal signal for the router.
111	
112	An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at chance. Episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below the majority-vote baseline of 50.0\%. Training uses episode-level splits (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode leakage. The high uninformative rate connects to noisy-label learning~\citep{natarajan2013learning}: standard ERM on labels with ${\sim}$88\% noise yields near-chance classifiers, consistent with our results.
113	
114	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Death-proximal labeling (training only on steps within $k$ steps of death events) is a promising direction for extracting step-level signal from episode-level outcomes. Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}).
115	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe introduced an episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents, overcoming per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate) through paired pure-strategy comparison with penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics. Applied to ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry---robust across four intervention axes, cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\\%$), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld). The diagnostic value is practical: our 200 GPU-hour router training could not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, an outcome a 50-episode pilot would have predicted.\n\nThe framework applies to any environment with episode-level evaluation and binary catastrophic outcomes. _source line 4563 · 2026-05-19T17:02:05.724Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch — 撤回 death-proximal labeling 结果]

Research Reviewer 审查发现 death-proximal 实验有 class imbalance confound（0% internal recall 是因为 86-90% deterministic 类别失衡，不是 noise reduction 无效的直接证据）。写入论文会被 reviewer 攻击。

**请做以下修改**：

1. **删除**刚加入的 death-proximal filtering 段落（"To directly test Interpretation A, we trained routers on death-proximal subsets..."那段）

2. **恢复** "Both interpretations are consistent with our data" 句式，改为：
"The data scaling ablation (§3.7, 25%–800% with no improvement) and the extreme class imbalance of death-asymmetry episodes (86/95 = 90.5% deterministic-preferred) together favor Interpretation B: routing signal is genuinely task-type-level, not step-level learnable. Definitive attribution between label noise and fundamental non-learnability remains an open question."

3. **不要**提及 death-proximal labeling（既不作为结果也不作为 future work）

4. 确保页面仍 ≤8 页。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VuKCBrw823T9m4yPtpWnmV`</summary>

```text
107	\subsection{Router Training: A Negative Result}
108	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
109	
110	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. The near-chance step-level accuracy admits two interpretations: (A)~episode-level labels projected onto steps create ${\sim}$88\% uninformative signal (60.3\% pre-event neutral, 27.4\% post-event), drowning learnable patterns in noise; (B)~routing signal is genuinely task-type-level---there is no step-level pattern to learn. Directional conflict is only 0.3\% of 2{,}040 training steps (labels are directionally correct in 99.7\% of informative steps), but the granularity mismatch leaves minimal signal for the router. To directly test Interpretation~A, we trained routers on death-proximal subsets---retaining only steps within $k \in \{1,3,5,10\}$ of a fatal action. If label dilution were the bottleneck, concentrating on informative steps should improve accuracy. Instead, all configurations yield internal recall of 0\% and balanced accuracy of 50\%, identical to the full-dataset baseline. The failure is structural: death-asymmetry episodes are inherently single-class (all labeled deterministic), so filtering uninformative steps cannot create internal-preferred training signal. Combined with data scaling (25\%--800\%, no improvement), death-proximal filtering and data scaling both favor Interpretation~B: routing signal is genuinely task-type-level, not step-level learnable.
111	
112	An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at chance. Episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below the majority-vote baseline of 50.0\%. Training uses episode-level splits (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode leakage. The high uninformative rate connects to noisy-label learning~\citep{natarajan2013learning}: standard ERM on labels with ${\sim}$88\% noise yields near-chance classifiers, consistent with our results.
113	
114	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}).
115	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01F6NpE2HLgHLAYGCKwj1SQK`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced an episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents, overcoming per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate) through paired pure-strategy comparison with penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics. Applied to ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry---robust across four intervention axes, cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\%$), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld). The diagnostic value is practical: our 200 GPU-hour router training could not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, an outcome a 50-episode pilot would have predicted.
4	
5	The framework applies to any environment with episode-level evaluation and binary catastrophic outcomes. Death-proximal labeling---concentrating training on steps near fatal actions---yields no improvement (internal recall 0\% across all $k$), confirming that step-level signal is absent in this regime. Future work should target intermediate death regimes (NetHack combat tasks) and extend to mixed-strategy routing and stronger backbones.
6	
```

</details>

14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate) in sparse-reward environments, enabling routing signal decomposition (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism dominates routing signal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\%$), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
16	
17	
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "561c3b2c-4709-4f5d-bf24-b0d88f2fc8d6",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n2\t\n3\t\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n4\t\n5\t\\usepackage{times}\n6\t\\usepackage{latexsym}\n7\t\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n8\t\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n9\t\\usepackage{microtype}\n10\t\\usepackage{graphicx}\n11\t\\graphicspath{{figures/}}\n12\t\\usepackage{booktabs}\n13\t\\usepackage{amsmath}\n14\t\\usepackage{amssymb}\n15\t\\usepackage{algorithm}\n16\t\\usepackage{algorithmic}\n17\t\n18\t\\title{Death Avoidance as the Dominant Routing Signal:\\\\A Counterfactual Analysis on ScienceWorld}\n19\t\n20\t\\author{Anonymous}\n21\t\n22\t\\begin{document}\n23\t\\maketitle\n24\t\n25\t\\input{abstract}\n26\t\\input{introduction}\n27\t\\input{method}\n28\t\\input{experiments}\n29\t\\input{related_work}\n30\t\\input{discussion}\n31\t\\input{conclusion}\n32\t\n33\t\\section*{Limitations}\n34\t\n35\t\\paragraph{Environmental scope.}\n36\tOur primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct cross-model replication (11 task types, $N{=}220$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) shows death dominance replicates with direction consistency 10/10 while vulnerability magnitude varies ($+30.0$~pp vs.\\ Qwen $+26.5$~pp), though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work. Our quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\\%$, label fractions) are specific to ScienceWorld; the framework's generality claim is methodological (the diagnostic procedure applies to any environment with asymmetric fatal outcomes), not empirical (the specific percentages may differ across environments).\n37\t\n38\t\\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}\n39\tOur internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\\% to 31.4\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). However, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label); the remaining apparent mislabeling consists of episode labels applied to steps where neither strategy has a step-level advantage (60.3\\% pre-event neutral). The issue is granularity mismatch---applying correct-direction labels to uninformative steps---not directional error. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Under mixed-strategy routing with oracle death prediction, switching to deterministic at the step before death could reduce the internal death rate; even under optimistic assumptions (perfect step-level death prediction), a residual gap of ${>}10$~pp between strategies is expected, as fatal trajectories involve cumulative hallucination errors preceding the death event. More broadly, projecting episode-level labels onto 10--50 sequential steps with path-dependent state introduces compounding error: early routing decisions alter the state trajectory, potentially invalidating later labels derived from the episode outcome.\n40\t\n41\t\\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}\n42\tEpisodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.\n43\t\n44\t\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n45\t\n46\tThis work uses ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All evaluation environments (ScienceWorld, ALFWorld, MATH) are released under permissive open-source licenses (Apache 2.0 / MIT). All models used (Qwen2.5 family, Llama-3.1-8B) are publicly available for research use. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. Experiments were conducted on 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 (32GB) and 4$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX 6000 PRO (96GB), totaling approximately 200 GPU-hours for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.\n47\t\n48\t\\bibliography{references}\n49\t\n50\t\\appendix\n51\t\n52\t\\noindent\\textbf{Appendix Overview.}\n53\t\\hyperref[app:sci]{A.~Signal Concentration Index} $\\cdot$\n54\t\\hyperref[app:labeling]{B.~Labeling Quality Audit} $\\cdot$\n55\t\\hyperref[app:per-task]{C.~Per-Task Decomposition} $\\cdot$\n56\t\\hyperref[app:correlation]{D.~Correlation Robustness} $\\cdot$\n57\t\\hyperref[app:wild-cluster]{E.~Wild Cluster Bootstrap} $\\cdot$\n58\t\\hyperref[app:per-task-ci]{F.~Per-Task CIs} $\\cdot$\n59\t\\hyperref[app:loto-daf]{G.~Leave-One-Task-Out DAF} $\\cdot$\n60\t\\hyperref[app:dose-response]{H.~Dose-Response Breakdown} $\\cdot$\n61\t\\hyperref[app:router-details]{I.~Router Training Details} $\\cdot$\n62\t\\hyperref[app:seed-robustness]{J.~Seed Robustness} $\\cdot$\n63\t\\hyperref[app:penalty]{K.~Penalty Sensitivity} $\\cdot$\n64\t\\hyperref[app:cross-env-details]{L.~Cross-Environment Details} $\\cdot$\n65\t\\hyperref[app:cross-model]{M.~Cross-Model Verification} $\\cdot$\n66\t\\hyperref[app:failure-modes]{N.~Failure Mode Analysis} $\\cdot$\n67\t\\hyperref[app:grounding-ablation]{O.~Grounding Ablation} $\\cdot$\n68\t\\hyperref[app:ablations]{P.~Ablation Studies} $\\cdot$\n69\t\\hyperref[sec:threshold-routing]{Q.~Threshold Routing} $\\cdot$\n70\t\\hyperref[app:decision-framework]{R.~Decision Framework}\n71\t\n72\t\\section{Signal Concentration Index}\n73\t\\label{app:sci}\n74\t\n75\tLet $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:\n76\t\\begin{equation}\n77\t    c_k = \\frac{n_k \\cdot \\delta_k}{\\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \\cdot \\delta_j}\n78\t\\end{equation}\n79\twhere $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\\delta_k = d_k^{\\tt int} - d_k^{\\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential.     "snippet": "1\t\\section{Experiments}\n2\t\n3\tWe analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset, not to all ScienceWorld tasks. An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\\Delta\\text{DR} = +38.2$\\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n4\t\n5\t\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n6\t\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n7\t\n8\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n9\t\\centering\n10\t\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n11\t\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$ (Spearman's $\\rho = 0.986$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}), $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types. Since $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ with $P$ constant, $r \\to 1$ as DAF $\\to 1$; the near-perfect correlation is a structural consequence of death dominance, not an independent finding (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).}\n12\t\\label{fig:decomposition}\n13\t\\end{figure}\n14\t\n15\tOf the 95 non-tie routing labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal). At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.\n16\t\n17\tOf 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\\%) are ties---neither strategy outperforms the other---indicating that routing is relevant in only 38.8\\% of episodes. Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$; $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\to 0$, DAF $\\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\\geq}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.\n18\t\n19\t\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\n20\tThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n21\t\n22\t\\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}\n23\tThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels, while \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores) produces 0\\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\\% and DAF to 96.8\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}).\n24\t\n25\t\\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}\n26\tNon-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for the death-avoidance label fraction, independent of cluster assumptions, with Cohen's $d = 3.02$ (interpret cautiously given $K{=}11$ task types). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}); an independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). As a conservative check, wild cluster bootstrap treating each task type as a cluster yields $p = 0.002$; however, with $K{=}11$ clusters this test falls below the $K{\\geq}20$--$40$ threshold recommended for reliable inference~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}, so we rely on the non-clustered bootstrap as primary. All 28 death-prone tasks share the same direction (Appendices~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\\ref{app:per-task-ci}).\n27\t\n28\t\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n29\t\\label{sec:cross-model}\n30\t\n31\tA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} cross-model replication across all 11 task types ($N{=}220$) confirms that death dominance replicates across model families: internal death rate $41.4\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $11.4\\%$ (death-rate differential $+30.0$~pp), DAF $= 88.2\\%$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +33.17$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +3.9$), with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks, 83 deterministic-preferred, 3 internal-preferred (1.4\\%, all death-asymmetry reversals), and 134 no-preference labels.\n32\tBoth models exhibit nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%: Qwen $42.9\\%$, Llama $41.4\\%$), confirming that death-prone failure is structurally determined; the differential magnitude varies ($+26.5$~pp Qwen, $+30.0$~pp Llama) because deterministic strategy safety is model-dependent ($16.3\\%$ vs.\\ $11.4\\%$).\n33\t\\texttt{identify-life-stages-1} shows cross-model precision matching ($55\\%$/$10\\%$/$+45$~pp in both models).\n34\t\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} shows zero deaths in both models, consistent with its majority-class answer distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\\times}$ scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) confirms that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n35\t\n36\t\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n37\t\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n38\t\n39\t\\begin{table}[t]\n40\t\\centering\n41\t\\small\n42\t\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n43\t\\toprule\n44\tCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n45\t\\midrule\n46\tSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\n47\tReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\n48\tQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\n49\tDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n50\t\\midrule\n51\t\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\n52\tDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\n53\tRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n54\t\\bottomrule\n55\t\\end{tabular}\n56\t\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks (72B: independently collected at both internal and deterministic). Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded, 0\\% death at all scales). All interventions reduce death rate but none approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n57\t\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n58\t\\end{table}\n59\t\n\n... [56 lines truncated] ..."

118	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types for analysis. 144	\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. D\%: death rate for internal (int) and deterministic (det) strategies. D/I/N: episode labels (det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference). Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}
145	\label{tab:per-task}

147	
148	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
149	Eighteen of the 19 excluded task types (95\%) exhibit death-asymmetric patterns (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}): internal-reasoning death rates range from 40\% to 60\% (mean 53.9\%), while deterministic death rates range from 0\% to 40\% (mean 15.8\%), yielding a mean death-rate differential of $+38.2$\,pp, exceeding the $+28.7$\,pp for included tasks. The included subset is thus \emph{less} death-asymmetric than the excluded subset, the opposite of what a cherry-picking hypothesis would predict. The sole exception is \texttt{grow-plant}, an intrinsically safe task with zero deaths under both strategies, paralleling \texttt{find-non-living-thing} in the included set. The 19 excluded tasks fall into three independently motivated categories: five used during pipeline development (standard hold-out practice), seven taxonomically redundant with included family representatives (deduplication), and seven where the 7B backbone achieves near-zero task progress regardless of strategy (agent capability floor). In no case was a task excluded because death dominance was absent. Exclusion is driven by absence of non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ for 53\% of excluded tasks) and standard deduplication practices. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: zero deaths under both strategies yet included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).
150	
151	\begin{table}[t]

    "oldString": "(1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate) in sparse-reward environments, enabling routing signal decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf})",
    "newString": "(1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate) and produces three interpretable outputs: penalty-independent label fractions, a Death Avoidance Factor decomposition, and oracle degeneracy detection (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}), enabling practitioners to assess routing value from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot before investing in router training",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone---the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated)---while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. The shared assumption---that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---has not been tested: no prior work has decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's penalty ($P{=}100$), DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$). The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic death ($-100$ penalty); once death episodes are removed, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$ (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate) in sparse-reward environments, enabling routing signal decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism dominates routing signal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\\%$), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 6,

Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).",
          " An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
          " ",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "3bf253e2-2306-424c-a69d-f2c706f7ef75",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "9159d1fd-ba77-458d-8b6f-339efb6638de",

    "oldString": "96.3\\% of oracle decisions favor always-deterministic",
    "newString": "96.3\\% of oracle decisions match always-deterministic",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), its practical implications were not predictable a priori. Existing routing work (Toolformer, CogRouter, ART, When2Tool, RouteLLM) implicitly assumes diverse signals; our finding that 78.9\\% of labels trace to a single mechanism contradicts this assumption. Intervention resistance confirms non-triviality: all four axes fail to eliminate death dominance (72B DAF${}= 92.4\\%$; Reflexion DAF${}>100\\%$ with $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$; action filtering leaves ${\\geq}12$~pp residual gap; threshold routing with perfect death knowledge cannot outperform always-deterministic). Cross-model stability (Qwen $+26.5$~pp, Llama $+30.0$~pp, both ${\\sim}42\\%$ internal DR) suggests death vulnerability is a task-environment property, not model-specific. Precise quantification (78.9\\%, not ``most'') enables principled deployment decisions; more aggressive interventions remain untested, so we characterize a ceiling, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}: zero deaths despite high submission rate). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practical value of routing.}\nThe framework reveals that 96.3\\% of oracle decisions favor always-deterministic, with 61.2\\% of episodes showing no strategy preference---routing is nearly unnecessary on ScienceWorld. This diagnostic finding matters because it prevents wasted computation: a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions, while our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding the always-deterministic baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: no death mechanism yields no signal; universal death lacks exploitable asymmetry. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",

Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).",

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), its practical implications were not predictable a priori. Existing routing work (Toolformer, CogRouter, ART, When2Tool, RouteLLM) implicitly assumes diverse signals; our finding that 78.9\\% of labels trace to a single mechanism contradicts this assumption. Intervention resistance confirms non-triviality: all four axes fail to eliminate death dominance (72B DAF${}= 92.4\\%$; Reflexion DAF${}>100\\%$ with $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$; action filtering leaves ${\\geq}12$~pp residual gap; threshold routing with perfect death knowledge cannot outperform always-deterministic). Cross-model stability (Qwen $+26.5$~pp, Llama $+30.0$~pp, both ${\\sim}42\\%$ internal DR) suggests death vulnerability is a task-environment property, not model-specific. Precise quantification (78.9\\%, not ``most'') enables principled deployment decisions; more aggressive interventions remain untested, so we characterize a ceiling, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}: zero deaths despite high submission rate). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practical value of routing.}\nThe framework reveals that 96.3\\% of oracle decisions match always-deterministic, with 61.2\\% of episodes showing no strategy preference---routing is nearly unnecessary on ScienceWorld. This diagnostic finding matters because it prevents wasted computation: a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions, while our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding the always-deterministic baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: no death mechanism yields no signal; universal death lacks exploitable asymmetry. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",
        "numLines": 18,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 18
      }
    },

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone---the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated)---while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. The shared assumption---that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---has not been tested: no prior work has decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's penalty ($P{=}100$), DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$). The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic death ($-100$ penalty); once death episodes are removed, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$ (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate) and produces three interpretable outputs: penalty-independent label fractions, a Death Avoidance Factor decomposition, and oracle degeneracy detection (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}), enabling practitioners to assess routing value from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot before investing in router training; (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism dominates routing signal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\\%$), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",
        "numLines": 18,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 18
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset, not to all ScienceWorld tasks. An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\\Delta\\text{DR} = +38.2$\\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$ (Spearman's $\\rho = 0.986$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}), $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types. Since $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ with $P$ constant, $r \\to 1$ as DAF $\\to 1$; the near-perfect correlation is a structural consequence of death dominance, not an independent finding (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOf the 95 non-tie routing labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal). At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.\n\nOf 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\\%) are ties---neither strategy outperforms the other---indicating that routing is relevant in only 38.8\\% of episodes. Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$; $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\to 0$, DAF $\\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\\geq}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels, while \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores) produces 0\\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\\% and DAF to 96.8\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}).\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}\nNon-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for the death-avoidance label fraction, independent of cluster assumptions, with Cohen's $d = 3.02$ (interpret cautiously given $K{=}11$ task types). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}); an independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). As a conservative check, wild cluster bootstrap treating each task type as a cluster yields $p = 0.002$; however, with $K{=}11$ clusters this test falls below the $K{\\geq}20$--$40$ threshold recommended for reliable inference~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}, so we rely on the non-clustered bootstrap as primary. All 28 death-prone tasks share the same direction (Appendices~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\\ref{app:per-task-ci}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} cross-model replication across all 11 task types ($N{=}220$) confirms that death dominance replicates across model families: internal death rate $41.4\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $11.4\\%$ (death-rate differential $+30.0$~pp), DAF $= 88.2\\%$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +33.17$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +3.9$), with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks, 83 deterministic-preferred, 3 internal-preferred (1.4\\%, all death-asymmetry reversals), and 134 no-preference labels.\nBoth models exhibit nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%: Qwen $42.9\\%$, Llama $41.4\\%$), confirming that death-prone failure is structurally determined; the differential magnitude varies ($+26.5$~pp Qwen, $+30.0$~pp Llama) because deterministic strategy safety is model-dependent ($16.3\\%$ vs.\\ $11.4\\%$).\n\\texttt{identify-life-stages-1} shows cross-model precision matching ($55\\%$/$10\\%$/$+45$~pp in both models).\n\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} shows zero deaths in both models, consistent with its majority-class answer distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\\times}$ scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) confirms that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks (72B: independently collected at both internal and deterministic). Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded, 0\\% death at all scales). All interventions reduce death rate but none approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters; both internal and deterministic strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\\%$ death rate ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (7B: $90.4\\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance intact.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp over five trials). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $z = 2.75$, $p = 0.003$) then plateaus ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). With $n{=}200$ paired episodes, the minimum detectable effect is ${\\sim}8$~pp ($\\alpha = 0.05$, 80\\% power): the key $N{=}1{\\to}3$ decline is well-powered; the plateau claim ($N{=}3{\\to}5$, $+1.0$~pp) is also supported, since a ${\\geq}8$~pp improvement would be detected. We characterize this as suggestive evidence of a Reflexion ceiling rather than a conclusive demonstration. Retry's per-trial conditional death rate stays constant at ${\\sim}48\\%$; Reflexion's declines to ${\\sim}31\\%$ by Trial~3 (per-task breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate as a function of trial count ($N$) for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Retry (blue) is nearly flat; Reflexion (red) drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus. Horizontal lines: 72B scaling baseline ($30.6\\%$, all 11 tasks) and deterministic baseline ($16.3\\%$). Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe examine MATH and ALFWorld as boundary probes---not cross-domain validation---testing framework applicability across environments that instantiate ``deterministic computation'' differently (oracle simulator, code interpreter, admissible-action enumeration).\n\n\\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$), with 74.6\\% no-preference. Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions---an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. Of 37 non-tie labels, 35 (94.6\\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy, a one-directional dominance driven by information asymmetry rather than catastrophic failure. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 20--40 episodes per type, 245 total). $\\dagger$\\,Single random seed (42) per episode pair; see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness analysis.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training: A Negative Result}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. The near-chance step-level accuracy admits two interpretations: (A)~episode-level labels projected onto steps create ${\\sim}$88\\% uninformative signal (60.3\\% pre-event neutral, 27.4\\% post-event), drowning learnable patterns in noise; (B)~routing signal is genuinely task-type-level---there is no step-level pattern to learn. Directional conflict is only 0.3\\% of 2{,}040 training steps (labels are directionally correct in 99.7\\% of informative steps), but the granularity mismatch leaves minimal signal for the router. The data scaling ablation (25\\%--800\\%, no improvement) and the extreme class imbalance of death-asymmetry episodes (86/95 $= 90.5\\%$ deterministic-preferred) together favor Interpretation~B: routing signal is genuinely task-type-level, not step-level learnable. Definitive attribution between label noise and fundamental non-learnability remains an open question.\n\nAn implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at chance. Episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline of 50.0\\%. Training uses episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode leakage. The high uninformative rate connects to noisy-label learning~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: standard ERM on labels with ${\\sim}$88\\% noise yields near-chance classifiers, consistent with our results.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).\n",
        "numLines": 115,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 115
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe introduced an episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents, overcoming per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate) through paired pure-strategy comparison with penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics. Applied to ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry---robust across four intervention axes, cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\\%$), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld). The diagnostic value is practical: our 200 GPU-hour router training could not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, an outcome a 50-episode pilot would have predicted.\n\nThe framework applies to any environment with episode-level evaluation and binary catastrophic outcomes. Future work should target intermediate death regimes (NetHack combat tasks), develop methods to disentangle label noise from fundamental non-learnability in step-level routing, and extend to mixed-strategy routing and stronger backbones.\n",

        "startLine": 1,

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U8c2DBssNKySSCCZvjmf7k`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.
5	\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.
6	\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.
7	\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.
8	\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.
9	All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
10	Concurrent work examines \emph{when} to invoke tools: \citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} find tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty; \citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states; \citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax''; \citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity-utility-affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
13	Adaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.
14	RouteLLM \citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.
15	These optimize \emph{how} to route; we reveal \emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.
16	
17	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}
18	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.
19	Our approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \emph{before} any router is trained.
20	
21	\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}
22	ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary failure mechanism. ReAct \cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \cite{lats-2024} adds tree search.
23	
24	\paragraph{Safe RL.}
25	Death avoidance as an implicit routing signal connects to safe RL. Constrained MDPs \citep{altman1999constrained} formalize safety through state or cost constraints; our mechanism can be viewed as an implicit absorbing-state constraint. Risk-sensitive RL \citep{mihatsch2002risk} optimizes CVaR-type objectives; our framework diagnoses when such risk-sensitivity emerges rather than prescribing it. Classical methods \citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} require explicit constraint specification; our routing achieves safety implicitly through computation mode selection. \citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category, consistent with our finding. SafeRoute \citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails.
26	
```

  "uuid": "d9daa9ef-8a94-46b0-820a-dd264931ef9c",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "These optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.",
    "newString": "These optimize \\emph{how} to route; our framework reveals \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\n\\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.\n\\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.\n\\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \\cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.\n\\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nConcurrent work examines \\emph{when} to invoke tools: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} find tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty; \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states; \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax''; \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity-utility-affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nDeath avoidance as an implicit routing signal connects to safe RL. Constrained MDPs \\citep{altman1999constrained} formalize safety through state or cost constraints; our mechanism can be viewed as an implicit absorbing-state constraint. Risk-sensitive RL \\citep{mihatsch2002risk} optimizes CVaR-type objectives; our framework diagnoses when such risk-sensitivity emerges rather than prescribing it. Classical methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} require explicit constraint specification; our routing achieves safety implicitly through computation mode selection. \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category, consistent with our finding. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails.\n",

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. _source line 4693 · 2026-05-19T19:03:15.905Z_

[Director 指令] **[Patch] Claim 强度提升 — 3 条具体重写**

Reviewer 做了 claim 强度深度评估，发现 predictive power 严重 underplayed，C2 缺 significance framing。以下 3 条重写总计 ~80 words 改动，不改论文结构。

**重写 1：Introduction C1 贡献句（introduction.tex，L15 附近）**
当前 C1 已经有 Paper Agent 之前加的 "three interpretable outputs" 版本。需要进一步 sharpen——把 "enabling practitioners to assess" 改为更具体的 predictive power 声明。

改为类似：
```latex
(1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework}
that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate),
decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent (label fraction)
and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics, and predicts routing value
from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (validated 4/4 conditions;
Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework})
```

关键强化：从 "enabling assessment"（模糊承诺）变为 "predicts, validated 4/4"（具体验证数据）。确保 Appendix reference 正确指向 Practitioner Decision Framework（当前标签可能是 app:decision-framework 或 app:practitioner-framework，请确认）。

**重写 2：Introduction C2 贡献句（introduction.tex，L15 附近）**
当前 C2 只说 "78.9% from death asymmetry"。加 intervention resistance + literature assumption challenge。

改为类似：
```latex
(2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that
\textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death
asymmetry---a single mechanism that persists across four
intervention axes and three model scales, contradicting the
routing literature's implicit assumption of diverse signal sources
(\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement})
```

关键强化："contradicting the routing literature's implicit assumption" 把 finding 从 "我们发现了 X" 升级为 "X 挑战了现有假设"——这是 Excitement 维度的关键句。

**重写 3：Abstract 最后一句（abstract.tex）**
当前版本："The framework diagnoses when catastrophic failure makes routing signal degenerate, enabling practitioners to assess routing value before investing in router training."

改为类似：
```latex
A three-stage diagnostic pilot (${\sim}$50 episodes) correctly
predicts routing outcomes across all four tested conditions,
demonstrating practical utility before any router is trained.
```

关键强化：从模糊承诺变为具体验证数据（"4/4 correct" + "~50 episodes"）。

修完后编译确认。这次不用验证引用。注意保持 Abstract 总长度合理（不要因为加了这句导致超过半页）。
